- **2026-07-18** [Brent Crude Hits $88 as Iran Extends War From Hormuz to Kuwait Drinking Water](https://www.techtimes.com/articles/320922/20260718/brent-crude-hits-88-iran-extends-war-hormuz-kuwait-drinking-water.htm) — Tech Times (relevance 0.25)
  > Brent's 14-percent weekly surge reflects a multi-node infrastructure war, not a tanker harassment spike

Iran hit a Kuwait power and water desalination facility on Saturday -- the second strike on Kuwaiti desalination infrastructure in two days -- causing a fire, knocking multiple power generation units offline, and pushing Brent crude oil to $88.10 a barrel, a 4.6% single-day advance that brings Brent's weekly gain to more than 14 percent. With tanker crossings through the Strait of Hormuz reduced to just eight on Thursday -- from a pre-conflict daily total of more than 100 -- and Fed rate-hike odds for September sitting at 73 percent, the Persian Gulf war has formally expanded from a shipping-lane crisis into a civilian infrastructure war. Every mortgage holder, commodity consumer, and equity investor in a rate-sensitive sector now has a direct financial stake in what happens at Kuwait's coastline.

West Texas Intermediate futures gained a corresponding 4.5%, closing at $82.49. The advance marks Brent's third consecutive weekly gain and its highest settlement since before the U.S.-Iran ceasefire collapsed in early July.

Iran Struck Kuwait Twice in Two Days -- Here Is Why That Matters

Kuwait's Shuaiba power and water desalination complex in Al Ahmadi Governorate was struck by Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps on Saturday, triggering a fire that Kuwait's Fire Force teams worked for hours to contain. Kuwait's Ministry of Electricity, Water and Renewable Energy confirmed the attack and activated emergency contingency plans. Iran simultaneously struck a Kuwait Petroleum Corporation oil facility, injuring several workers.

This is the second attack on Kuwaiti desalination infrastructure in two days. The first occurred on Friday. Kuwait briefly closed its airspace Saturday morning due to missile threats, and Kuwait Airways announced it was rescheduling most flights to and from the capital.

The timing of the attacks is deliberately punishing. The Gulf region is currently experiencing temperatures exceeding 100°F, and desalinated water is not a convenience in Kuwait -- it is survival infrastructure. More than 90 percent of the country's drinking water comes from desalination plants processing seawater from the Gulf. The Center for Strategic and International Studies described GCC desalination plants in a 2026 analysis as "large, fixed, open-air industrial complexes mostly concentrated along the coast within 350 kilometres of the Islamic Republic," adding that they are "as exposed to Iranian weaponry as any civilian infrastructure."

Iran had explicitly threatened this category of strike for months. After Trump threatened Iranian power stations, Tehran warned that Gulf desalination plants would be targeted in response. That reciprocal dynamic has now materialized: Saturday's attack on the Shuaiba complex, designated by Iran's military as Wave 15 of Operation Nasr-2, is the first confirmed strike on civilian water production infrastructure in the current conflict phase.

Hormuz Traffic at Eight Vessels -- Why Pipeline Alternatives Cannot Fill the Gap

The Strait of Hormuz -- the 34-kilometer-wide narrows through which roughly 20 percent of global oil supply and 20 percent of global liquefied natural gas trade once transited daily -- remains severely constricted. Trade intelligence firm Kpler recorded just eight commercial vessel crossings on Thursday, down from fifteen the prior day and from a pre-conflict daily total of more than 100. The shipping risk firm Marisks called the situation a "worst-case scenario" for oil tankers, with at least nine ships coming under attack in the strait since July 6.

Iran has made the waterway hostile through four mechanisms operating simultaneously: missile and drone attacks on vessels, sea mines (whose precise locations Iran has not fully tracked), satellite spoofing and GNSS jamming of navigation systems, and VHF radio warnings backed by demonstrated willingness to fire on non-compliant ships.

What prevents a diplomatic agreement from quickly normalizing prices is a specific engineering constraint: the pipeline alternatives that Saudi Arabia, the UAE, and Iraq have activated -- the East-West Crude Oil Pipeline to Yanbu, the Abu Dhabi Crude Oil Pipeline to Fujairah, and the Kirkuk-Ceyhan pipeline through Turkey -- have a combined capacity of approximately 9 million barrels per day. Hormuz carried roughly 20 million barrels per day before the conflict. No combination of existing pipelines closes that gap. Iraq and Kuwait, which have no pipeline alternatives at scale, face an indefinite bottleneck. Qatar -- which supplies approximately 20 percent of global LNG -- has effectively halted LNG carrier voyages.

The IEA responded by unlocking 400 million barrels from member-country strategic petroleum reserves in March -- the largest such release in the institution's history. That intervention achieved a partial moderating effect. It did not reopen the strait.

Seventh Night of U.S. Strikes; Houthi Threat Signals Second Chokepoint

U.S. Central Command confirmed early Saturday that American forces had completed their seventh consecutive night of strikes against Iranian military targets -- hitting "surveillance sites, military logistics infrastructure, underground weapons storage, and maritime capabilities." CENTCOM previously reported more than 50,000 U.S. service members operating across the region.

The U.S. struck bridges, an airport, and energy sites inside Iran on Friday, and separately hit an electricity and desalination facility in Iran's southern Hormozgan province. Trump's repeated threats to target Iranian power stations appear to have generated precisely the retaliation Iranian officials had warned would follow: the Shuaiba complex strikes.

Beyond Kuwait, Iran's Saturday operations hit Qatar, Jordan, and Kurdish communities in Iraq. Armed men seized a vessel near Yemen, signaling that Houthi forces in the Red Sea may be preparing to reactivate attacks on commercial shipping -- potentially compressing both of the world's primary maritime energy corridors simultaneously.

The six months of conflict since Operation Epic Fury on February 28, 2026 have produced the following documented toll: at least 17 merchant ships damaged, two captured, and 12 seafarers killed or missing. Fourteen U.S. service members have been killed and 427 wounded since the war began; Iranian authorities say U.S. strikes have killed at least 50 people and wounded more than 500 in Iran.

What the $88 Price Signals: This Is Not a Peak, It May Be a Floor

Market analysts have drawn a consistent distinction between the current oil rally and prior Gulf tension spikes. In 2019, Iran's harassment campaign and the Abqaiq refinery attack produced sharp but brief premiums because the shipping lanes remained nominally open. The current situation differs structurally: multiple supply nodes are being hit simultaneously, and the strait is functionally closed rather than merely threatened.

The $88.10 settlement Friday is the highest Brent close since the U.S.-Iran ceasefire collapsed in early July -- but it is still roughly 30 percent below Brent's April 30, 2026 intraday high of $126.41. The ceasefire drove prices back toward $80. The current re-escalation -- now including civilian water infrastructure -- is the mechanism pushing prices back toward the upper range. Analysts who forecast $100/bbl if Hormuz disruptions persisted have not yet been proven wrong.

Fed September Rate-Hike Odds at 73%: What Borrowers Need to Know Now

The oil shock has moved decisively beyond the energy sector into the broader macroeconomic debate. CME FedWatch futures markets put the probability of a Federal Reserve rate hike at the September 16, 2026 FOMC meeting at 73% as of July 16 -- up from just 26% in mid-June.

That repricing reflects a compound problem. Core Personal Consumption Expenditures -- the Fed's preferred inflation gauge -- reached 3.4% in May, its 63rd consecutive month above the Fed's 2% target. May CPI came in at 4.2% year-over-year before falling to 3.5% in June, driven by a 0.4% single-month decline. That disinflationary relief is now being erased by the Brent crude re-acceleration.

The inflationary effects of the conflict have spread beyond gasoline. Energy cost pass-through into plastics, polymers, supply-chain rerouting, and AI infrastructure has broadened the inflationary base into demand-driven territory -- the channel rate hikes can actually address. The Federal Reserve's June minutes identified AI hardware demand as an independently inflationary pressure: overwhelming GPU and memory demand is driving hardware costs higher in ways that have nothing to do with oil but that respond to the same monetary tightening.

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh has maintained a hawkish posture throughout. Governor Christopher Waller warned in a July 13 speech that the Fed must not repeat the mistakes of 2021 and 2022 by waiting too long to raise rates. The current federal funds target range is 3.50% to 3.75%, where it has held since the June 17 meeting. Nine of nineteen FOMC participants projected at least one rate hike before year-end at that June meeting.

With Brent at $88.10 -- $4 above Thursday's close that already had markets repricing September -- the most immediate decision for borrowers, home buyers, and rate-sensitive investors is whether the Fed's posture hardens further before July 28. That FOMC decision will tell markets whether the June inflation relief survives the current oil move or gets overwritten by it.

How Can Oil Prices Rise When Hormuz Has Been Closed for Months?

The short answer is that prices are responding not to the Hormuz closure itself -- which markets have partially priced since February -- but to qualitative escalations that change the ceiling on how bad this gets. Saturday's strikes on civilian water infrastructure represent a new category of escalation: from disrupting energy exports to targeting the systems that keep Gulf city populations alive during extreme summer heat. That shift raises the political temperature, lengthens the expected duration of the conflict, and reduces the probability of a near-term ceasefire capable of reopening the strait.

The $88 close also reflects a market that has now seen the ceasefire-and-reopen cycle fail once: prices fell toward $80 during the June MOU, then reversed sharply when Iran re-closed the strait in early July. Traders are now pricing a lower probability of durable ceasefire, which keeps the supply-risk premium elevated.

Frequently Asked Questions

Will the Federal Reserve raise rates in September 2026?

As of July 16, CME FedWatch put the probability of a September 16 FOMC rate hike at 73%, up from 26% in mid-June. The conflict-driven inflation has spread beyond oil into demand-driven components -- plastics, GPU hardware, supply-chain rerouting -- that rate hikes can address. Nine of nineteen FOMC participants already projected a 2026 hike at the June meeting. The July 28-29 FOMC decision will be the first real indication of whether the June inflation data or the current oil re-acceleration holds more weight for the committee.

What is Kuwait's Shuaiba desalination plant, and why is its destruction significant?

Shuaiba is a large coastal power and water desalination complex in Kuwait's Al Ahmadi Governorate. Like all Gulf desalination plants, it sits within demonstrated range of Iranian ballistic and cruise missiles. More than 90 percent of Kuwait's drinking water comes from facilities like Shuaiba, with no viable terrestrial freshwater alternatives. Striking it during summer temperatures above 100°F is not a military tactic -- it is a humanitarian pressure mechanism targeting civilian water supplies, which raises questions about compliance with international humanitarian law's protections for infrastructure essential to civilian survival.

How much higher could oil prices go if the strait stays closed?

Brent crude peaked at $126.41 during the acute phase of the conflict in late April before the June ceasefire brought prices toward $80. At $88.10, prices are approximately 30 percent below that peak. The structural constraint is the pipeline gap: existing alternative pipelines can move roughly 9 million barrels per day, while Hormuz normally carried 20 million. Until that gap closes -- through Hormuz reopening, new pipeline capacity, or severe demand destruction -- every sustained escalation puts pressure on the upper end of the range. Analysts at Goldman Sachs had forecast $100/bbl if Hormuz disruptions persisted; that scenario has not been disproven.

Is the Red Sea now at risk of closing too?

Iran has reportedly instructed Houthi forces in Yemen to prepare to disrupt Red Sea shipping if the U.S. targets Iranian power infrastructure. Saturday's seizure of a vessel near Yemen is a possible indicator of Houthi re-engagement. If Houthi attacks resume at scale, they would close the Bab el-Mandeb strait -- the Middle East's other critical maritime chokepoint -- potentially cutting off the alternative routing that some Gulf energy has been using during the Hormuz crisis. That scenario would represent the most severe simultaneous maritime chokepoint compression in the history of global energy trade.

In a stark warning following the seventh night of US strikes against Iran, the regimes Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC) said that the country's Gulf neighbors must be ready to receive a corresponding and proportional response.

In the statement, the IRGC warned the host nations with US military bases to activate their civil defense units and relocate civilians away from potential military targets, Irans semi-official Tasnim news agency reported on July 18.

The IRGC warning came hot on the heels of an apparent shift in US military strategy, with strikes expanding from targets near the Strait of Hormuz to multiple sites across central and southern Iran on July 18.

Quoting the IRGC, Irans ISNA news agency reported that the groups's fresh warning is Irans direct response to a shift in the US military strategy over the past 24 hours.

Referring to the ongoing US strikes in its statement, the IRGC justified its warning by stating that Iran is executing the reciprocal defense following the failure of international bodies to halt these actions.

On July 17, the US Central Command (CENTCOM) said the fresh strikes are designed to continue degrading Iranian military capabilities at the direction of the commander in chief (US President Donald Trump).

CENTCOM said it "hit surveillance sites, military logistics infrastructure, underground weapons storage, and maritime capabilities employing fighter aircraft, aerial drones, and warships in addition to other assets.

Earlier, Irans top military officials had threatened full-scale offensive operations if the US continued striking Iran for another two or three days. Quoting Major General Mohsen Rezaei, who is a senior military adviser to Irans Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei, Iranian news agency IRIB reported that "Iran will no longer limit itself to retaliatory, like-for-like responses...and no political border will be safe."

Meanwhile, Tehran continued firing drones and missiles toward neighboring Bahrain, Iraq, Kuwait, Oman, and Qatar on July 17, resulting in damage to infrastructure.

Al-Jazeera reports that the security threat level was elevated in Qatar amid sounds of loud explosions in the capital, Doha, early on the morning of July 17. Quoting the Qatari Interior Ministry, the TV channel said a child was injured by falling shrapnel during the attacks.

Qatar hosts a major US military base and has been the target of regular Iranian drone and missile strikes since the beginning of the conflict in late February.

In Kuwait, the countrys Electricity, Water, and Renewable Energy Ministry said in a July 17 statement that a power and water desalination plant was hit in the Iranian strikes that sparked a fire, damaging a large number of electricity generation units.

As hostilities continue with risk of further expansion of the war, there are fresh calls for an immediate cease-fire and a resumption of dialogue.

On July 17, the foreign ministers of China and Pakistan, in a joint appeal, called for a return to the cease-fire agreed upon between Iran and the United States last month.Share

02:35

Hormuz Shipping Comes To Near Halt Amid Latest Flareup Of Violence

Shipping through the Strait of Hormuz -- through which some 20 percent of the worlds crude oil and gas supplies transited prewar -- has almost ground to a halt again following the renewed exchange of attacks, according to data supplier Kpler.

Only eight ships passed through the strait on July 16, the lowest figure in three weeks and down from 15 passages a day earlier and from 48 two weeks ago,Kpler said.

"Seven of the eight transits used the Iranian route, highlighting a growing concentration of movements through higher risk corridors as operators reassess security, crew safety, and insurance exposure," it said.

UAE-IRAN-US-ISRAEL-WAR

"While regional mediation efforts remain under way, commercial shipping patterns suggest military developments are now shaping operational decisions more than diplomatic progress."

Prewar, more than 100 vessels on average passed through the waterway each day.

The reduction in transit traffic has led to major shortages of oil and gas throughout the world and pushed energy prices sharply higher.

Prices had dipped and traffic increase following an interim cease-fire agreement between Washington and Tehran, but the latest attacks by both sides has again disrupted shipment of supplies.Share

Exiled Iranian Kurdish Group Reports 9 Killed In Suspected Iranian Attack

At least nine members of an Iranian Kurdish armed opposition group based in Iraq's Kurdistan region were killed on July 17 on an attack the exiled party blamed on Iran.

Idriss Kohlwazi of the exiled Komala Party of Iranian Kurdistan told AFP: "The Iranian regime attacked at 4:30 a.m. (0130 GMT) with drones and rockets a camp."

The strikes killed nine members of the party at their camp near the city of Sulaimaniyah, he said.

The Iraqi government also blamed the attack on Iran.

The attacks come amid renewed military escalation between the US and Iran.

Thousands of self-exiled Iranian Kurds have established bases in the semiautonomous Iraqi Kurdistan region. The Iranian Kurds, although keeping ties, maintain separate militias from the Iraqi Kurds in the region.With reporting by AFPShare

- **2026-07-18** [BKS Supports Ethanol, Demands GST on Fuel](https://www.newkerala.com/news/a/mp-bks-backs-ethanol-dismisses-water-use-concerns-seeks-298.htm) — newKerala.com (relevance 0.25)
  > The Bharatiya Kisan Sangh (BKS) has strongly backed the Centre's ethanol blending programme, dismissing allegations of excessive water consumption. BKS National President K. Sai Reddy stated that ethanol production increases farmers' income and reduces dependence on imported crude oil. The organization has demanded that petrol and diesel be brought under GST to ensure uniform fuel prices. Additionally, BKS urged for hassle-free MSP procurement and natural farming adoption, while also meeting CM Mohan Yadav over the Kailaras Sugar Mill.

MP: BKS backs ethanol, dismisses water-use concerns; seeks GST on fuel

Bhopal, July 18

Addressing a press conference here, BKS National President K. Sai Reddy said misinformation was being spread about ethanol and claimed it helps increase farmers' income, reduce dependence on imported crude oil and curb pollution.

He said opposing ethanol is against the interests of farmers and the nation, as it boosts income, reduces dependence on foreign oil and helps control pollution.

Rejecting criticism over water use, Reddy said the claim that ethanol production wastes thousands of litres of water is completely false.

He explained that only a limited amount of water is used to extract juice from sugarcane, and the water used in factories is recycled.

Reddy added that ethanol produced from crops such as sugarcane, maize and rice provides farmers with better returns while contributing to the country's energy security. He also favoured promoting vehicles capable of running entirely on ethanol.

Regarding concerns that ethanol‑blended petrol affects vehicle performance, he said such fuel has been in use in the country for the past five years. If vehicles develop technical problems, manufacturers should address them instead of blaming ethanol, he added.

The BKS also demanded that petrol and diesel be brought under the Goods and Services Tax (GST) regime, saying it would ensure uniform fuel prices across the country and provide relief to consumers.

The farmers' body further sought hassle‑free procurement of crops at the minimum support price (MSP), increased procurement of moong and early release of the Madhya Pradesh government's share under the crop insurance scheme so that pending claims are settled without delay.

Reddy also appealed to farmers to adopt natural farming to reduce cultivation costs.

Earlier in the day, a BKS delegation met Chief Minister Mohan Yadav and demanded that the Kailaras Sugar Mill in Morena be handed over to farmers through a cooperative society instead of being privatised. It also proposed constituting a committee of farmers, shareholders and technical experts under the district collector to manage the mill.

According to the organisation, the Chief Minister assured the delegation that the proposal would be considered.
- **2026-07-18** [Aliko Dangote secures $2.5 billion private share placement for his refinery](https://www.ghanaweb.com/GhanaHomePage/business/Aliko-Dangote-secures-2-5-billion-private-share-placement-for-his-refinery-2043835) — GhanaWeb (relevance 0.25)
  > Aliko Dangote has nearly completed a $2.5 billion private stock placement for his refinery business, according to people familiar with the matter, as the company prepares for Africa's largest initial public offering.

Africa's richest person sold a stake representing as much as 6% of Dangote Petroleum Refinery & Petrochemicals FZE at a pricing that would value the company at about $40 billion, one of the people said, asking not to be identified discussing confidential matters.

The fundraising comes ahead of a planned IPO that could raise an additional $1.5 billion to $2 billion, with a public listing expected as early as August.

Business Insider Africa previously reported that Dangote intends to list the refinery on the Nigerian Exchange as part of a broader effort to deepen local participation in one of Africa's most strategic industrial assets.

The billionaire has consistently advocated for greater African ownership of major infrastructure projects, a strategy reflected in the strong participation of regional investors during the private placement.

The refinery also recently secured $750 million through a debt offering, further strengthening its capital base.

The 650,000 barrels-per-day refinery, which began production in 2024, has expanded output across diesel, aviation fuel, naphtha and petrol, significantly reducing Nigeria's reliance on imported refined petroleum products.

The latest fundraising will help finance an ambitious expansion that aims to more than double the refinery's processing capacity to 1.4 million barrels per day by 2028, positioning it among the world's largest refining complexes.

Dangote is also considering building a refinery in Kenya worth between $15 billion and $17 billion as part of efforts to reduce Africa's reliance on imported petroleum products.

The proposed facility, with an estimated processing capacity of up to 700,000 barrels of crude oil per day, would become the largest refinery in East Africa if completed.
